# Imperially Compiled Balanced Interpretations of the Zhou Changes

## Juan Twenty · 卷二十

The Introduction to the Study of the Changes (易學啓蒙), Parts Three and Four: the yarrow-stalk procedure, changing-line interpretation, and all thirty-two transformation charts. [Chinese source](../source/juan-20.md) · [Translation conventions](README.md)

[Part Three](#part-three) · [Part Four](#part-four) · [Transformation charts](#transformation-charts) · [Translator’s Endnotes](#translators-endnotes)

**Voices:** Primer text and glosses are distinguished from canonical quotations, the collected commentators, and the imperial compilers’ Editorial Judgments. The concluding Editorial Judgment offers an alternative to the primer’s multiple-changing-line rules. Descriptive headings and chart entry numbers are reading aids. Twelve chart image references and two chart names are corrected in the English, with linked endnotes and a precise manifest; the Chinese source and all image files are unchanged.

<a id="eee-4715"></a>

<a id="part-three"></a>

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:001 -->

**Part Three · Explaining the Yarrow Stalks**[^j20-scope]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:002 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** The number of the Great Expansion is fifty.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:003 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The central number of both the River Chart and the Luo Writing is five. Extend it, bringing each of its numbers to its limit of ten, and together they make fifty. The River Chart’s numbers total fifty-five. Of these, fifty are obtained through five; five alone is that upon which the fifty depend, while itself depending on nothing else. Leave it empty, and only fifty remain. Again, of the fifty-five, forty are apportioned as the numbers of old and young yin and yang, while five and ten have no assigned function. Multiply five by ten, or ten by five, and each also makes fifty. The Luo Writing’s numbers total forty-five. Forty are distributed outside and apportioned as the numbers of old and young yin and yang; five alone occupies the center without an assigned function. It therefore contains a further five within itself, making fifty in all.[^j20-fifty]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:004 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The Great Plan says, “Five for turtle divination and two for stalk divination, to work out departures from the norm.” “Work out” means extend and trace; “departures” means errors and deviations. Turtle and stalk divination trace the errors and deviations of human affairs; hence the method of counting yarrow is called the Great Expansion. “Great” is pronounced tai, as in Grand Diviner and Grand Stalk-Diviner: it is an expression of honor, not what earlier scholars meant by lesser and greater expansion. Explanations of fifty differ; only those that trace it to the Chart and Writing are sound. The River Chart has five in excess: the constitution of number. The Luo Writing lacks five: the activity of number. Does not the Great Expansion take the mean between the River and Luo numbers, embracing the full principles of constitution and activity?

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:005 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** Of these, forty-nine are used.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:006 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The number of the Great Expansion is fifty, while one yarrow root bears a hundred stalks, enough for two sets of that number. Thus the method takes fifty stalks as one handful and sets one aside, unused, to image the Supreme Ultimate. The stalks to be used number forty-nine: an image of the Two Modes complete in constitution but not yet divided.[^j20-hanging]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:007 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:008 -->

**Cui Jing:** “Of these, forty-nine are used” models the seven-times-seven number of growing yang. Since the sixty-four hexagrams model the eight-times-eight number of growing yin, the forty-nine stalks model growing yang’s seven times seven. The yarrow is round and numinous, imaging Heaven; the hexagrams are square and knowing, imaging Earth. This is the distinction between yin and yang. The one set aside, unused, images the Supreme Ultimate’s emptiness and nonaction.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:009 -->

**Master Shao:** In the yarrow’s working numbers, one is hung to image the Three Powers. The remaining forty-eight are a figure’s stalks: four times twelve is forty-eight. Remove three from twelve and use nine: four times three, twelve, are the stalks removed; four times nine, thirty-six, those used. Remove five from twelve and use seven: four times five, twenty, are removed; four times seven, twenty-eight, used. Remove six from twelve and use six: four times six, twenty-four, are removed; four times six, twenty-four, used. Remove four from twelve and use eight: four times four, sixteen, are removed; four times eight, thirty-two, used. Thus seven and nine are yang, six and eight yin. Nine is yang’s limiting number, six yin’s. At the limit, number reverses; thus these bring about the figure’s change.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:010 -->

**Master Shao:** The remainder numbers reach their limit at four, and five is not used; the stalk numbers reach their limit at nine, and ten is not used. Thus fifty is left aside and forty-nine used.[^j20-remainder]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:011 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** Divide them in two to image the Two Modes; hang one to image the Three Powers; count them by fours to image the four seasons; lodge the remainders between the fingers to image intercalation. In five years there are two intercalations; therefore lodge twice, and then hang again.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:012 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Hanging means suspending a stalk in the little-finger gap. Counting means separating stalks between thumb and index finger. The remainder is the number left over; lodging means holding it in the two gaps between the three middle fingers. There are forty-nine stalks. Divide them freely in the middle, holding a portion in each hand to image the Two Modes. Hang one stalk from the right-hand portion in the left hand’s little-finger gap to image the Three Powers. Then count the left-hand stalks by fours to image the four seasons, and lodge their remainder in the left hand’s fourth-finger gap to image intercalation. Count the right-hand stalks by fours and lodge their remainder again, in the left hand’s third-finger gap, to image a second intercalation. *(The image of five years: hanging one is the first; counting the left portion, the second; lodging its remainder, the third; counting the right portion, the fourth; lodging its remainder, the fifth.)* This is one processing round. The hanging stalk and lodged remainders total either five or nine.[^j20-finger-gaps]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:013 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** The River Chart’s central palace is the Supreme Ultimate; the Luo Writing’s central palace is the human ultimate. Thus, in the Great Expansion, the one left unused images the Supreme Ultimate’s nonaction, while the one hung images the human ultimate’s participation and assistance. After leaving one aside, dividing in two makes clear that differentiating yin and yang is the root of creative transformation. After hanging one, counting by fours and lodging the remainders makes clear that determining the seasons and completing the year are the framework of human affairs. Dividing in two and hanging one establish Heaven and Earth’s positions, with humanity standing between them: the constitution of the Three Powers is complete. Counting by fours and lodging remainders set the four seasonal qi in alternating motion, with the five phases variously interwoven. The hundred kinds of things are born, the myriad affairs arise, and the activity of the Three Powers proceeds. In this way the Great Expansion takes the mean between the River and Luo and embraces the fullness of constitution and activity.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:013 -->

<a id="four-outcomes"></a>

#### The Four Outcomes

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:014 -->

![First round: the three remainder arrangements totaling five](../assets/eee-learning/30c3d498e791-le1.png) — *First round: the three remainder arrangements totaling five.*[^j20-diagram-key]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:015 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Three arrangements yield five: these are called odd.[^j20-case-counts]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:016 -->

**Primer gloss.** Subtract the one hanging stalk from five and four remain. Divide by four and the result is one; hence odd—the yang number of the Two Modes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:017 -->

![First round: the single remainder arrangement totaling nine](../assets/eee-learning/89a2ad6b2831-le2.png) — *First round: the single remainder arrangement totaling nine.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:018 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** One arrangement yields nine: this is called even.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:019 -->

**Primer gloss.** Subtract the one hanging stalk from nine and eight remain. Divide by four and the result is two; hence even—the yin number of the Two Modes.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:020 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** After the first round, remove its remainders and reunite the stalks still present, either forty or forty-four. Divide, hang, count, and lodge as before: this is the second round. The hanging stalk and lodged remainders total either four or eight.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:021 -->

![Later rounds: the two remainder arrangements totaling four](../assets/eee-learning/2c614445a4df-le3.png) — *Later rounds: the two remainder arrangements totaling four.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:022 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Two arrangements yield four: these are called odd.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:023 -->

**Primer gloss.** Do not subtract the hanging stalk; the rest has the same meaning as before.[^j20-retain-hanging]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:024 -->

![Later rounds: the two remainder arrangements totaling eight](../assets/eee-learning/08d6b95b1d53-le4.png) — *Later rounds: the two remainder arrangements totaling eight.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:025 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Two arrangements yield eight: these are called even.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:026 -->

**Primer gloss.** Do not subtract the hanging stalk; the rest has the same meaning as before.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:027 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** After the second round, remove the remainders of the preceding two rounds and reunite the stalks still present, either forty, thirty-six, or thirty-two. Divide, hang, count, and lodge as before: this is the third round. Its hanging stalk and lodged remainders follow the same rule as the second round.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:028 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Once three rounds are complete, consider them together. Examine whether their hanging stalks and lodged remainders belong to the odd or even classes, thereby distinguishing the old and young yin or yang encountered. This constitutes one line.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:029 -->

![Twelve formal cases producing old yang](../assets/eee-learning/f37fe1fc9177-le5.png) — *Twelve formal cases producing old yang.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:029 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:030 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The three odd results shown here produce old yang in twelve cases. The hanging stalks and lodged remainders total thirteen. Subtract the initial hanging stalk and twelve remain. Divide by four and apportion among the three rounds: there are three ones. One, being odd, images a circle with circumference three; thus each of the three ones contains a further three. Three threes make nine. The counted-through stalks number thirty-six; divided by four, these too yield nine.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:030 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:031 -->

**Primer gloss.** The hanging stalks and lodged remainders, less one, take one of the four quarters of forty-eight: once twelve, or three fours. This is nine’s mother. The counted-through stalks take three of the four quarters of forty-eight: three twelves, or nine fours. This is nine’s child. Both image diameter one and circumference three.[^j20-mother-child]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:031 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:032 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** This is the number of great yang among the Four Images, occupying position one and containing nine.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:032 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:033 -->

![Twenty-eight formal cases producing young yin](../assets/eee-learning/cae209188ab8-le6.png) — *Twenty-eight formal cases producing young yin.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:033 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:034 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The two odd results and one even shown here take the even as their governing element and produce young yin in twenty-eight cases. The hanging stalks and lodged remainders total seventeen. Subtract the initial hanging stalk and sixteen remain. Divide by four and apportion among the three rounds: there are two ones and one two. One, being odd, images the circle, whose whole is used; thus each of the two ones contains a further three. Two, being even, images the square, of which half is used; thus the one two contains a further two. Two threes and one two make eight. The counted-through stalks number thirty-two; divided by four, these too yield eight.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:034 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:035 -->

**Primer gloss.** The hanging stalks and lodged remainders, less one, are four fours: an advance of four from once twelve. This is eight’s mother. The counted-through stalks are eight fours: a retreat of four from three twelves. This is eight’s child.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:035 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:036 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** This is the number of young yin among the Four Images, occupying position two and containing eight.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:036 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:037 -->

![Twenty formal cases producing young yang](../assets/eee-learning/e8807f148e76-le7.png) — *Twenty formal cases producing young yang.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:037 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:038 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The two even results and one odd shown here take the odd as their governing element and produce young yang in twenty cases. The hanging stalks and lodged remainders total twenty-one. Subtract the initial hanging stalk and twenty remain. Divide by four and apportion among the three rounds: there are two twos and one one. Two, being even, images the square, of which half is used; thus each of the two twos contains a further two. One, being odd, images the circle, whose whole is used; thus the single three contains a further three. Two twos and one three make seven. The counted-through stalks number twenty-eight; divided by four, these too yield seven.[^j20-one-three]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:038 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:039 -->

**Primer gloss.** The hanging stalks and lodged remainders, less one, are five fours: a retreat of four from two twelves. This is seven’s mother. The counted-through stalks are seven fours: an advance of four from two twelves. This is seven’s child.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:039 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:040 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** This is the number of young yang among the Four Images, occupying position three and containing seven.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:040 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:041 -->

![Four formal cases producing old yin](../assets/eee-learning/ee3ff53c42d0-le8.png) — *Four formal cases producing old yin.*

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:041 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:042 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The three even results shown here produce old yin in four cases. The hanging stalks and lodged remainders total twenty-five. Subtract the initial hanging stalk and twenty-four remain. Divide by four and apportion among the three rounds: there are three twos. Two, being even, images the square, of which half is used; thus each of the three twos contains a further two. Three twos make six. The counted-through stalks also number twenty-four; divided by four, these too yield six.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:042 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:043 -->

**Primer gloss.** The hanging stalks and lodged remainders, less one, are six’s mother; the counted-through stalks are six’s child. Each takes two of the four quarters of forty-eight: two twelves, or six fours. Both image circumference four with half of it used.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:043 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:044 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** This is the number of great yin among the Four Images, occupying position four and containing six.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:044 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:045 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:045 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:046 -->

**Cai Yuanding:** The yarrow’s remainder combinations are twelve old yang, four old yin, twenty young yang, and twenty-eight young yin: sixty-four in all. Thirty-two are yang—twelve old and twenty young. Thirty-two are yin—four old and twenty-eight young. Sixteen belong to old yang and old yin: twelve and four. Forty-eight belong to young yang and young yin: twenty and twenty-eight. Old yang and old yin image Qian and Kun: two eights. Young yang and young yin image the six children: six eights.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:046 -->

<a id="method-disputes"></a>

#### Disputes over the Counting Method

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:047 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** All four outcomes are obtained by the method of hanging in each of the three rounds. The Classic says, “lodge twice, and then hang again,” and “four operations complete a change”: its meaning is entirely clear. Though the annotations and subcommentaries do not explain it fully, Liu Yuxi’s account of the monk Yixing, Bi Zhonghe, and Gu Tuan already sets it forth. Recent scholars, however, say that one hangs only in the first round, not in the next two. Measured against the Classic, this would mean lodging six times before hanging again, which does not accord with the image of “two intercalations in five years.” Moreover, the latter two rounds would have only three operations. The error is plain.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:047 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:048 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:048 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:049 -->

**Hu Fangping:** Wang Fusi’s annotation says: dividing in two is the first operation; hanging one to image the Three Powers, the second; counting by fours, the third; lodging the remainders, the fourth. Kong Yingda’s subcommentary says: “Lodge twice, and then hang again” means that, having divided Heaven into the left hand and Earth into the right, one counts Heaven’s number four by four, lodging the final remainder together in one place for the hanging stalk and lodged remainders. This is one lodging. Then one counts Earth’s number four by four, joining its final remainder to the stalks already lodged and holding them together. This is “lodge twice, and then hang again.”

Liu Yuxi’s Discussion Distinguishing Nine and Six in the Changes says that Bi Zhonghe’s learning derived from Chan Master Yixing. Yixing’s Fundamental Discussion of the Great Expansion Calendar, composed in the Tang Kaiyuan period, says: “Combine the numbers of excess and deficiency; in five years there are two intercalations.” Its method of expansion thus always lodges twice and then hangs again. Bi Zhonghe’s counting method expressly hangs in all three rounds, exactly fitting the meaning of four operations. Zhu Xi likewise says that Bi’s counting method is more detailed than the subcommentary; Gu Tuan’s account has not been fully ascertained. Yuxi himself further says: in the first finger-position, if one remains, add three; if two remain, add two; if three remain, add one; if four remain, add four. In the second finger-position, if one remains, add two; if two remain, add one; if three remain, add four; if four remain, add three. The third finger-position follows the second. This makes clear that all three rounds include hanging.[^j20-add-remainders]

Among recent scholars, Guo Yong’s Clarifying Doubts about Yarrow and Hexagrams specifically hangs only in the first round, not in the next two. It records Master Hengqu as saying, “‘Lodge twice, and then hang again’ means hanging after each line has been completed”—that is, not hanging in the second and third countings. Guo further claims that Hengqu’s words reveal the error of the annotations and subcommentaries. Zhu replies: “This account is gravely mistaken; I fear these are not Hengqu’s words. ‘Lodging twice’ means that within a single round one counts both left and right portions and lodges twice. One hanging, two countings, and two lodgings correspond to five years. The hanging and the two countings represent the years without intercalation; the two lodgings represent the two intercalary years. ‘Then hang again’ means that, after one round is complete, one reunites the remaining stalks, divides them in two, and hangs one to begin the next round. To say instead that hanging occurs in the first round but not in the second and third is to treat the round that includes hanging as ‘hanging’ and an image of intercalation, and the rounds without hanging as ‘lodging’ and images of nonintercalary years. This does not correspond at all to the Great Commentary’s ‘hang one to image the Three Powers’ and ‘lodge twice to image intercalation.’ Moreover, it leaves the first round’s two lodgings uncounted, calls the second and third rounds the ‘two lodgings,’ and gives those rounds only three operations, falling short of the number required to complete a change. Its numbers for old and young yin and yang also fail to agree in many respects.”

Guo records Master Yichuan as saying, “Again divide them between the left and right hands, without hanging another odd stalk.” Zhu replies: “This account is still more doubtful. Yet Guo says there was originally no written text, so errors in its oral transmission would hardly be surprising.”

Guo also says, “Even without hanging, the second and third countings still yield variations of four and eight; hanging is therefore unnecessary.” Zhu replies: “There are two reasons why hanging cannot be omitted. Of the three rounds, the first belongs to yang, so its remainders, five and nine, are both odd numbers. The latter two belong to yin, so their remainders, four and eight, are both even numbers. What belongs to yang has three yang cases and one yin, all following circumference three and diameter one. What belongs to yin has two yin cases and two yang, all following circumference four with half used. These are obtained by hanging in all three rounds; without hanging in the latter two, they cannot be obtained. After three rounds there are twelve possibilities for old yang, four for old yin, twenty-eight for young yin, and twenty for young yang. Although their numbers differ, each has its model and image. This too results from hanging in all three rounds and cannot be obtained without hanging in the latter two. Guo has seen only the single fact that the second and third rounds can proceed without hanging, and has seized on it as his explanation. How could he know that the difference between hanging and not hanging has these consequences for correctness and error? In general, although many of his other explanations are one-sided and rigid, his method does not greatly diverge. Here alone the discrepancy, though small, seriously impairs the formation of hexagrams and changing lines. It must therefore be distinguished with particular care.”

I have examined the matter. Hanging only in the first round, not in the next two, means not merely six lodgings before hanging and only three operations completing a change, at odds with “two lodgings” and “four operations.” Although the latter two rounds still give four or eight, they do so differently. With hanging, a total of four results from a left remainder of one and right remainder of two, or left two and right one. Without hanging, it results from left one and right three, left two and right two, or left three and right one. This is the difference in how four is obtained. With hanging, eight results from left four and right three, or left three and right four. Without hanging, both left and right must leave four. This is the difference in how eight is obtained. After three rounds, the numbers of yin and yang transformations are unevenly assorted and no longer preserve their spontaneous models and images.[^j20-voices]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:049 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:050 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Furthermore, using the old method, the first of the three rounds is odd and the latter two even. Being odd, the first leaves five or nine; being even, the latter leave four or eight. Among the five-or-nine remainders, five has three cases and nine one: this too expresses circumference three and diameter one. Among the four-or-eight remainders, four and eight each have two cases: this too expresses circumference four with half used. After three rounds, among the old, yang is abundant and yin scant; among the young, yang is fewer and yin more. Each likewise has its spontaneous model and image.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:050 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:051 -->

**Cai Yuanding:** Take fifty yarrow stalks, leave one unused, divide in two, hang one, and count by fours. There are three odd cases and two even: the spontaneous number of Heaven’s three and Earth’s two.[^j20-cai-three-two] Across the three countings, old yang and old yin would originally each have eight cases, making sixteen together. Old yin and yang are moving, but yin’s nature is fundamentally still; thus four are transferred to old yang. This is why old yin has four cases and old yang twelve. Young yang and young yin would originally each have twenty-four, making forty-eight together. Young yin and yang are still, but yang’s nature is fundamentally active; thus four are transferred to young yin. This is why young yang has twenty cases and young yin twenty-eight. Yang uses the old, not the young; thus, of the sixty-four transformations, sixteen are used.[^j20-yang-old] Reduce these by four, and yang uses three while yin uses one. A single odd and a single even standing opposite one another are yin and yang’s constitution; three yang and one yin, one abundant and one scant, are their activity. Thus spring, summer, and autumn generate things, while winter does not. East, west, and south are visible, while north is not. Human vision likewise sees ahead and to left and right, but not behind. Otherwise, take forty-nine stalks, leave one aside, divide in two, hang one, and count by fours: odd and even would each have two cases, old yang and old yin eight each, young yang and young yin twenty-four each. Would this not also be good? Could the sages’ wisdom have failed to reach this? Their choice of the former rather than the latter was precisely because yin and yang’s numbers in constitution are always equal, whereas in activity yang has three and yin one.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:051 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:052 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:052 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:053 -->

**Su Shi:** The learning of Tang Master Yixing holds that when all three rounds leave fewer stalks, they image Qian. Qian is old yang; counting the stalks through by fours yields nine, so it is named nine. When all three rounds leave more, they image Kun. Kun is old yin; counting through by fours yields six, so it is named six. When only one of the three rounds leaves fewer, they image Zhen, Kan, and Gen. These are young yang; counting through by fours yields seven, so they are named seven. When only one round leaves more, they image Xun, Li, and Dui. These are young yin; counting through by fours yields eight, so they are named eight. Thus seven, eight, nine, and six name yin and yang according to the counting numbers. What makes yin and yang old or young, however, lies not in these numbers themselves, but in the images of the Eight Trigrams within the three rounds. This is Tang Master Yixing’s teaching.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:053 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:054 -->

**Collected Writings of Master Zhu:** “In the first round, three arrangements yield five and one yields nine; hence ‘among the five-or-nine remainders, five has three cases and nine one.’ In the next two rounds, two arrangements yield four and two yield eight; hence ‘among the four-or-eight remainders, four and eight each have two cases.’ After three rounds, twelve yield old yang and four old yin; hence ‘yang is abundant and yin scant.’ Twenty yield young yang and twenty-eight young yin; hence ‘yang is fewer and yin more.’ Shen’s Brush Talks says: ‘Among the Changes’ images, nine is old yang, seven young yang, eight young yin, and six old yin. These numbers all have their sources; they arise spontaneously, not through deliberate assignment. The returned remainders may be greater or fewer. Greater is yin, like the divided line; fewer is yang, like the undivided line. Three fewer results are Qian, hence old yang. They leave nine groups of four, so its number is nine and its stalks thirty-six. Two greater results and one fewer take the single fewer result as governing: these are Zhen, Kan, and Gen, all called young yang. The fewer result at the beginning is Zhen, in the middle Kan, and at the end Gen. Each leaves seven groups of four, so its number is seven and its stalks twenty-eight. Three greater results are Kun, hence old yin. They leave six groups of four, so its number is six and its stalks twenty-four. Two fewer results and one greater take the single greater result as governing: these are Xun, Li, and Dui, all called young yin. The greater result at the beginning is Xun, in the middle Li, and at the end Dui. Each leaves eight groups of four, so its number is eight and its stalks thirty-two.’

Of the various accounts of stalk-counting, the Brush Talks alone is both concise and complete. Kong Yingda did understand the counting method, but had not practiced it thoroughly; thus his account easily goes astray in its wording, though not in its principal totals. Bi Zhonghe is more detailed than the subcommentary. Liu Zihou was wrong to disparage Liu Mengde’s learning as superficial. Bi’s statement that one stalk is hung in all three countings exactly accords with the four operations. His only minor error is distributing the hanging stalks and lodged remainders of the three countings among three finger-gaps; the principal totals are still correct. The expressions ‘one remains: add three,’ and the like, are faults of Mengde’s excessively compressed wording. They lead the reader to suspect that the results depend on human intention rather than arising spontaneously. These expressions and Kong’s account must be corrected, yet their intentions should also be understood sympathetically.

When Cai speaks of taking forty-nine stalks, leaving one unused, dividing in two, hanging one, and counting by fours, he means that only forty-eight are used after the one is set aside. The remainders of dividing, hanging, and counting then have two odd cases and two even, with eight transformations each for old yang and old yin and twenty-four each for young yin and young yang: sixty-four in all, each of the Eight Trigrams receiving eight. But these are obtained by the opposed odd and even classes doubling; they are numbers of constitution. Heaven’s three and Earth’s two, extended to fifty, are numbers of activity. The numbers of constitution are always equal; those of activity give abundant yang and scant yin. This is precisely the subtlety of creative transformation. To put yin and yang in the same category and old and young under the same rule gives numbers of constitution, not of activity.”[^j20-counterfactual]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:054 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:055 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** If the recent method is used, the remainders of all three rounds express circumference three and diameter one, without the distinction between odd and even rounds. After three rounds, old yang and young yin each have twenty-seven cases, young yang nine, and old yin one. These are again unevenly assorted and no longer possess their spontaneous models and images. This suffices to reveal the error of that account.

Let us now discuss more comprehensively why yin and yang are old or young. There are forty-nine stalks. Subtract the initial hanging stalk and forty-eight remain; divide by four and the result is twelve, or divide by twelve and the result is four. Thus, in one counting round, a hanging-and-lodged total of one four is odd, and of two fours even. Across three rounds, when the hanging-and-lodged total is three fours, or once twelve, and the counted-through total nine fours, or three twelves, the result is old yang. When both totals are six fours, or two twelves, the result is old yin. Add one four to old yang’s hanging-and-lodged total: this makes four fours, or once twelve advanced by four. Subtract one four from its counted-through total: this makes eight fours, or three twelves diminished by four. This is young yin. Subtract one four from old yin’s hanging-and-lodged total: this makes five fours, or two twelves diminished by four. Add one four to its counted-through total: this makes seven fours, or two twelves advanced by four. This is young yang.[^j20-two-twelves]

The two old outcomes are yin and yang’s extremes. The distance between these extremes is twelve; divide it in three. From yang’s extreme, increase the hanging-and-lodged total and reduce the counted-through total, each by one third of that distance, and one obtains young yin. From yin’s extreme, reduce the hanging-and-lodged total and increase the counted-through total, each by one third, and one obtains young yang. Old yang occupies position one and contains nine: its hanging-and-lodged total of twelve is least, and its counted-through total of thirty-six greatest. Young yin occupies position two and contains eight: its hanging-and-lodged sixteen is next least, and its counted-through thirty-two next greatest. Young yang occupies position three and contains seven: its hanging-and-lodged twenty is somewhat greater, and its counted-through twenty-eight somewhat fewer. Old yin occupies position four and contains six: its hanging-and-lodged twenty-four is greatest, and its counted-through twenty-four least. Yang is odd and yin even; thus, in the hanging-and-lodged totals, old yang is least and old yin greatest, while the two young outcomes advance and retreat to meet between them. Here fewer is valued. Yang is solid and yin empty; thus, in the counted-through totals, old yang is greatest and old yin least, while the two young outcomes likewise advance and retreat to meet between them. Here more is valued.

In all this, not only do yin and yang, as two things, alternately wane and wax; within either one, these two ends each constitute something distinct and alternately wane and wax. They rise and fall against each other like a balance; they separate and fit together like the halves of a tally. Human private ingenuity cannot choose what to retain or discard, conjuring them into being or abolishing them. Moreover, the hanging-and-lodged numbers are the source of seven, eight, nine, and six, while the counted-through numbers are their downstream result. Their relative importance therefore differs. Some nevertheless wish to set aside the hanging-and-lodged counts and decide solely from the counted-through numbers. This abandons the root for the branch, the concise for the elaborate, without recognizing the impropriety. Is it not mistaken?[^j20-remainders-priority]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:055 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:056 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:056 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:057 -->

**Gui Youguang:** When nine is present in the counted-through stalks, three odd results appear in the remainders. When six is present, three even results appear. When seven is present, two even and one odd appear. When eight is present, two odd and one even appear. Without looking back at the counted-through total, one has already grasped the essential point. Why, then, does the text say, “Qian’s stalks number 216, Kun’s 144,” and “the stalks of the two parts number 11,520”? Although the hanging-and-lodged counts grasp the essential point, the numbers seven, eight, nine, and six still take the counted-through stalks as primary. Seven, eight, nine, and six are obtained by counting in fours; the hanging-and-lodged totals appear as a consequence of that counting. Thus it says, “count by fours to image the four seasons,” and again, “corresponding to the days of a year,” while lodging the remainders images intercalation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:057 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:058 -->

**He Kai:** The Wing says, “Count by fours to image the four seasons; lodge the remainders to image intercalation.” The four seasons are the regular measure; intercalation is the remainder. Below it says, “Qian’s stalks number 216, Kun’s 144; together they make 360, corresponding to the days of a year,” and “the stalks of the two parts number 11,520, corresponding to the number of the myriad things.” All begin their reckoning from seven, eight, nine, and six. It is clear that the regular numbers are used, not the remainder numbers.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:058 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:059 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Gui’s and He’s explanations may also be weighed alongside Zhu’s.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:059 -->

<a id="completing-hexagram"></a>

#### Completing the Hexagram

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:060 -->

**Primer quotation — Master Shao.** Five together with four and four, less the initial hanging stalk, makes three fours, twelve. Nine together with eight and eight, less that stalk, makes six fours, twenty-four. Five with eight and eight, or nine with four and eight, less that stalk, makes five fours, twenty. Nine with four and four, or five with four and eight, less that stalk, makes four fours, sixteen. Thus the numbers three, four, five, and six are removed to produce the stalk numbers nine, eight, seven, and six: this is what is meant. **Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Once a line is complete, reunite the forty-nine stalks and again divide, hang, count, and lodge to complete a round. Every three rounds complete one line, entirely according to the preceding method.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:060 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:061 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** Qian’s stalks number 216; Kun’s, 144. Together they make 360, corresponding to the days of a year.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:061 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:062 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Qian’s 216 stalks are obtained by adding the thirty-six stalks of each of its six lines. Kun’s 144 are obtained by adding the twenty-four stalks of each of its six lines. The total of 360 is obtained by combining 216 and 144. “Corresponding to the days of a year” means thirty days in each month, making 360 in twelve months. Reckoned through the solar terms there are 366 days; through the new moons, 354. The mean between solar excess and lunar deficiency is given here; thus it says 360. Yet young yang has twenty-eight stalks, making 168 for Qian’s six lines; young yin has thirty-two, making 192 for Kun’s six lines. Only the stalks of old yin and yang are mentioned here because the Changes uses nine and six, not seven and eight. Nevertheless, the two young totals likewise add to 360.[^j20-year]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:062 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:063 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** The stalks of the two parts number 11,520, corresponding to the number of the myriad things.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:063 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:064 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The two parts are the sixty-four hexagrams of the Upper and Lower Classics. Their 192 yang lines, each with thirty-six stalks, total 6,912. Their 192 yin lines, each with twenty-four stalks, total 4,608. Together these make 11,520. For young yang, each line has twenty-eight stalks, totaling 5,376; for young yin, each has thirty-two, totaling 6,144. Their sum too is 11,520.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:064 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:065 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** Thus four operations complete a change; eighteen changes complete a hexagram. The Eight Trigrams make the lesser completion. Draw this out and extend it, pursue its kinds and enlarge it, and all that can be accomplished in the world is fulfilled.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:065 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:066 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Four operations means four successive undertakings. Dividing in two is the first; hanging one, the second; counting by fours, the third; lodging the remainders, the fourth. “Change” means a transformation: one processing round of stalk-counting. Four operations complete a round; three rounds complete a line. One round gives the image of the Two Modes; two rounds, the image of the Four Images; three rounds, the image of the Eight Trigrams. One line gives a stroke of the Two Modes; two lines, the strokes of the Four Images; three lines, the strokes of the Eight Trigrams. When four lines are complete, one of the sixteen figures is obtained; when five are complete, one of the thirty-two. Seventy-two operations complete eighteen rounds, revealing six lines and yielding one of the sixty-four hexagrams. Yet already at thirty-six operations and nine rounds there are three strokes and the name of one of the Eight Trigrams can be seen: the inner trigram as zhen is established. This is “the Eight Trigrams make the lesser completion.” Proceeding from here, draw it out and extend it: a further thirty-six operations and nine rounds complete three more strokes, yielding another figure of lesser completion. The outer trigram as hui is then complete as well. With six lines formed and inner and outer trigrams complete, the distinction identifying one of the sixty-four hexagrams becomes apparent. Examine which lines change and which do not, then pursue their kinds and enlarge the interpretation: the good fortune, misfortune, regret, and remorse of the world’s affairs do not fall outside this.[^j20-operations]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:066 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:067 -->

**Primer quotation — Appended Statements.** It makes the Way manifest and virtue and conduct numinous. Thus one may exchange responses with it and assist the numinous powers.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:067 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:068 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The Way is made manifest through the words; conduct is made numinous through the numbers. “Exchanging responses” means the hidden and manifest answering one another, like host and guest in their intercourse. “Assisting the numinous powers” means having the means to aid the work of numinous transformation.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:068 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4715:069 -->

**Source note.** Cai’s statement in this juan that there are three odd cases and two even means that, in the first counting, left-hand remainders of one, two, and three all belong to the odd class, while four belongs to the even. In the second and third countings, a remainder of three also belongs to the even class; hence two odd cases and two even.[^j20-final-source-note]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4715:069 -->

<a id="eee-4716"></a>

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4716 -->

<a id="part-four"></a>

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:001 -->

**Part Four · Examining Change and Divination**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:002 -->

**Primer quotation — Qian and Kun.** Qian’s Using Nines: “A company of dragons appears, none at their head. Good fortune.” The Image says, “Using Nines: Heaven’s virtue must not take the lead.” Kun’s Using Sixes: “Enduring constancy in what is right is beneficial.” The Image says, “Using Sixes, enduring constancy: it ends with greatness.”[^j20-using]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:002 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:003 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Using Nines and Using Sixes are general rules for changing hexagrams. They mean that all yang lines use nine, not seven, and all yin lines use six, not eight. Using nine, old yang changes into young yin; using six, old yin changes into young yang. Seven and eight are not used, so young yang and young yin do not change. The rules are stated only under Qian and Kun because these stand at the beginning of the hexagrams and are pure yang and pure yin. The sage accordingly appended statements so that anyone obtaining Qian with all six lines nine, or Kun with all six lines six, could divine from them. “A company of dragons, none at their head” images all yang changing into yin; “enduring constancy is beneficial” expresses all yin changing into yang. For the rest, see the rule for six changing lines.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:003 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:004 -->

**Master Ouyang:** What do Qian’s Using Nines and Kun’s Using Sixes mean? Qian’s lines are seven or nine; Kun’s, eight or six. Nine and six change, while seven and eight remain inactive. The Way of the Changes divines through change; hence the lines are named by what is used in divination. This does not mean that all six lines are always nine or six. In casting, seven and eight are commonly more numerous, nine and six fewer. A cast may contain no nines or sixes at all; this must be explained. All sixty-four hexagrams are alike in this respect. Displaying the rule under Qian and Kun makes the rest knowable.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:004 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:005 -->

**Primer gloss.** I judge that this explanation illuminates what earlier scholars had not reached and is especially valuable. Its statement that seven and eight are more numerous, nine and six fewer, also shows that the divination method of that time hung in all three rounds, as Yixing taught.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:005 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:006 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When none of a hexagram’s six lines changes, divine from the original hexagram’s Judgment, taking the inner trigram as zhen and the outer as hui.[^j20-judgment]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:006 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:007 -->

**Primer gloss.** The Judgment is the statement beneath the hexagram. When Kong Chengzi cast to establish Yuan, a prince of Wei, he obtained Difficulty at the Beginning: “It is beneficial to establish a lord.” When the lord of Qin cast before attacking Jin, he obtained Decay: “Its zhen is wind; its hui, mountain.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:007 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:008 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When one line changes, divine from the statement of that changing line in the original hexagram.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:008 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:009 -->

**Master Cheng of Shasui:** Bi Wan obtained Difficulty at the Beginning changing to Holding Together: the first nine changed. Cai Mo’s Qian changing to Fellowship has the second nine changing. Duke Wen of Jin obtained Great Possession changing to Opposition: the third nine changed. Chen Jingzhong obtained Viewing changing to Obstruction: the fourth six changed. Nan Kuai obtained Kun changing to Holding Together: the fifth six changed. Duke Xian of Jin obtained The Marrying Maiden changing to Opposition: the top six changed.[^j20-historical-examples]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:009 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:010 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:010 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:011 -->

**Hu Yigui:** The Introduction says that when one line changes, one divines from its statement in the original hexagram, and cites Bi Wan’s cast below. Examined now, that example does not neglect the resulting hexagram. Nor does it discuss the one line alone: it also uses the configurations of zhen and hui. This seems fit to serve as a model for diviners. It is still clearer in Duke Xuan of Chen’s divination at the birth of Prince Wan.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:011 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:012 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When two lines change, divine from the statements of the two changing lines in the original hexagram, giving priority to the upper one.[^j20-inferred-rules]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:012 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:013 -->

**Primer gloss.** The Classic and commentaries contain no express statement. Inferred by analogy, this is how it should be.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:013 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:014 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:014 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:015 -->

**Hu Yigui:** Chen Tuan’s divination for Song Taizu also extended to other lines and the hexagram’s configuration.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:015 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:016 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When three lines change, divine from the Judgments of both the original and resulting hexagrams, taking the original as zhen and the resulting as hui. Among the first ten cases, give priority to zhen; among the latter ten, to hui.[^j20-three-lines]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:016 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:017 -->

**Primer gloss.** Three changing lines yield twenty possible hexagrams. A chart follows below.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:017 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:018 -->

**Master Cheng of Shasui:** When Chong’er, prince of Jin, cast concerning possession of the state, he obtained “Difficulty at the Beginning, with Enthusiasm as hui, all eights.” The first, fourth, and fifth lines changed: the first and fifth changed as nines, the fourth as six. The unchanged second, third, and top lines were eights in both hexagrams; hence “all eights.” The Minister of Works, Jizi, interpreted it: “Both favor establishing a lord.”

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:018 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:019 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:019 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:020 -->

**Hu Yigui:** The Introduction says only to consult the Judgments of the original and resulting hexagrams. Yet the lord of Jin’s cast of Difficulty at the Beginning and Enthusiasm clearly also consults their configurations.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:020 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:021 -->

**Xiong Penglai:** Seven and eight both denote unchanging lines. Why is seven seldom mentioned, while eight is singled out? Seven times seven is the number of the yarrow; eight times eight, the number of the hexagrams.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:021 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:022 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When four lines change, divine from the two unchanging lines in the resulting hexagram, giving priority to the lower one.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:022 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:023 -->

**Primer gloss.** Here too the Classic and commentaries contain no express statement. Inferred by analogy, this is how it should be.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:023 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:024 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When five lines change, divine from the one unchanging line in the resulting hexagram.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:024 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:025 -->

**Primer gloss.** When Mu Jiang went to the Eastern Palace, her cast yielded “Keeping Still’s eight.” The historian said, “This is Keeping Still changing to Following.” Five lines changed, while only the second obtained eight and remained unchanged. According to the rule, the oracle should have been “Tied to the little boy, one loses the grown man.” The historian wrongly cited Following’s Judgment in his reply; that was incorrect.[^j20-mujiang]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:025 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:026 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** When all six lines change, consult the two Using statements for Qian and Kun; for the other hexagrams, consult the Judgment of the resulting hexagram.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:026 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:027 -->

**Primer gloss.** Cai Mo’s statement, “Qian changing to Kun says, ‘A company of dragons appears, none at their head. Good fortune,’” is an example. Yet “a company of dragons, none at their head” is precisely Kun’s mare and its warning that taking the lead brings error. Kun’s “enduring constancy is beneficial” is precisely Qian’s not naming what it benefits.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:027 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:028 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** Thus one hexagram can change into sixty-four, and all 4,096 are contained within this. Is not the saying true: “Draw this out and extend it, pursue its kinds and enlarge it, and all that can be accomplished in the world is fulfilled”? We now arrange the transformations of the sixty-four hexagrams in thirty-two charts. One who obtains a chart’s first figure proceeds from beginning to end, from top to bottom. One who obtains its last figure proceeds from end to beginning, from bottom to top. For a transformation within the first thirty-two entries, consult the original hexagram’s line statements; beyond the thirty-second, consult the changed hexagram’s line statements.[^j20-chart-reading]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:028 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4716:029 -->

**Primer gloss.** “Beginning,” “end,” “top,” and “bottom” refer to the chart. Numbered positions before or after a given entry are counted from the original hexagram.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4716:029 -->

<a id="eee-4717"></a>

<!-- Source: https://www.eee-learning.com/book/4717 -->

<a id="chart-01"></a>

#### Chart 1 · Qian (1) ↔ Kun (2)

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4717:001 -->

**1.** ![Qian — hexagram 1](../assets/eee-learning/87922a56f976-yi01b.png) Qian (1)[^j20-chart-order]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4717:001 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4717:002 -->

**2.** ![Coming to Meet — hexagram 44](../assets/eee-learning/4237e42b256e-yi44b.png) Coming to Meet (44)  ·  **3.** ![Fellowship — hexagram 13](../assets/eee-learning/c41a60bb5c99-yi13b.png) Fellowship (13)  ·  **4.** ![Treading — hexagram 10](../assets/eee-learning/1d2f4126aa0a-yi10b.png) Treading (10)  ·  **5.** ![Small Restraint — hexagram 9](../assets/eee-learning/6c467b76b5af-yi09b.png) Small Restraint (9)  ·  **6.** ![Great Possession — hexagram 14](../assets/eee-learning/1aea54c6c468-yi14b.png) Great Possession (14)  ·  **7.** ![Breakthrough — hexagram 43](../assets/eee-learning/d1efb0ce8183-yi43b.png) Breakthrough (43)

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4717:002 -->

<a id="concluding-discussion"></a>

#### Concluding Discussion

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4717:577 -->

**Primer text — Zhu Xi.** The thirty-two charts above, read in both directions, become sixty-four charts. Each takes one hexagram as its starting figure and contains all sixty-four, making 4,096 in all, in agreement with Jiao Gan’s Forest of Changes. Yet the precision of their organization reveals something earlier scholars had not brought to light. Let the reader examine them closely.

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4717:577 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4717:578 -->

**Collected Explanations.**

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4717:578 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4717:579 -->

**Hu Yigui:** Jiao Yanshou’s method of hexagram transformation changes one hexagram into sixty-four; across all sixty-four, the transformations total 4,096. Their order follows King Wen’s sequence. For example, taking Qian as the original hexagram, its transformations begin with Kun, followed by Difficulty at the Beginning and Unformed Understanding, continuing through Before Completion. Taking the last hexagram, Before Completion, as the original, its transformations likewise begin with Qian, followed by Kun and Difficulty at the Beginning, continuing through After Completion. Each hexagram changes into sixty-three others; including the original makes sixty-four. Master Ziyang arranges the cases by the number of changing lines to determine the order of each hexagram’s transformations. He then extends the sequence of Qian’s transformations to establish the succession of transformations for all sixty-four hexagrams. The order is thereby fully articulated, each receiving its proper place. Though derived from Jiao, it is more precise than Jiao’s.[^j20-jiao]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4717:579 -->

<!-- BEGIN TRANSLATION: eee-4717:580 -->

**Editorial Judgment.** Zhu’s thirty-two charts are ordered with exceptional precision, yet later students have two doubts. First, the stalk method uses nine and six, not seven and eight. To divine, when four or five lines change, from the unchanging lines of the resulting hexagram is also to use seven and eight. Second, before the Duke of Zhou appended the line statements, the use of the hexagram Judgments would then have been incomplete.

The divination methods of the Three Dynasties have not been transmitted in full. We can now proceed only by inference from the Classic and its commentaries. Using Nine and Using Six are clearly stated in the Classic; no explicit statement in the various texts prescribes using seven and eight. Du Yu alone supposed that Xia and Shang used them, and earlier scholars have already exposed his error. Examining the Inner and Outer Commentaries to the Spring and Autumn Annals, we find that, whether lines change or not, and however many change, the interpretation always considers the hexagram’s configuration and images together with its Judgment. Even when one line changes, although its statement is consulted, priority is still given to the hexagram’s configuration, images, and Judgment.

We therefore know that, in ancient divination, the Judgments alone were already sufficient before the line statements existed. After the line statements were added, they were consulted when one line alone moved, and even then the decision never departed from the Judgment.

Since any one hexagram can change into sixty-four, the two figures can be considered together to exhaust the principles of affairs and things. Thus, when a hexagram changes, the intention is to generate a hexagram, not to constitute a line oracle. When lines change, a single moving line has a line oracle; several moving together do not. On this understanding, the transmitted texts agree and students’ doubts can be resolved.[^j20-compilers-alternative]

The Inner and Outer Commentaries mention obtaining eight in three cases: “Peace’s eight,” where there is no change; “Difficulty at the Beginning as zhen, Enthusiasm as hui, all eights,” where three lines change; and “Keeping Still’s eight, Keeping Still changing to Following,” where five lines change. The scholars’ interpretation of eight as an unmoving line does not seem to fit the wording. Although these three divinations differ in their number of changes, none has a line moving alone; all are alike in using the hexagram. Hexagrams are completed through eight, so eight identifies a hexagram, just as lines are constituted through nine and six, which therefore identify lines. Readers of Zhu’s charts must also compare the Zuo Tradition and the Discourses of the States.[^j20-eight]

<!-- END TRANSLATION: eee-4717:580 -->

<a id="translators-endnotes"></a>

## Translator’s Endnotes

The base is the unchanged repository transcription. Textual and chart checks used [the Siku Quanshu transcription, fixed revision 759597](<https://zh.wikisource.org/w/index.php?title=御纂周易折中_(四庫全書本)/卷20&oldid=759597>) and the historical page images identified below. Corrections supported by witnesses, contextual clarification, retained problematic readings, and interpretive disagreement are distinguished. All 2,056 image positions are retained; twelve chart references are corrected rather than silently reproducing erroneous glyphs. This is not a systematic leaf-by-leaf facsimile collation.

[^j20-scope]: This juan contains Parts Three and Four of the Introduction to the Study of the Changes (易學啓蒙), together with the thirty-two transformation charts and the imperial compilers’ concluding discussion. Primer text, quotations, glosses, collected explanations, and Editorial Judgments are distinct layers; an Editorial Judgment is never the translator’s voice. The compilation’s separate front matter and preliminary juan are not translated here.

[^j20-fifty]: The several derivations of fifty are the primer’s symbolic explanations, not competing instructions to take different quantities of stalks. The River Chart totals 55; the Luo Writing totals 45; the working set begins with 50 and leaves one aside. The compilers read 大 as tài, an honorific “great,” while rejecting a technical opposition of lesser and greater expansion. Their quotation from the Great Plan compresses the five turtle-omen categories and two stalk-divination categories.

[^j20-hanging]: Two different removals must not be confused. One stalk is set aside from the fifty before a line is produced and is not among its forty-nine working stalks. A further stalk is taken from the right-hand portion and “hung” between fingers at the beginning of each processing round in Zhu Xi’s method. Each line requires three such rounds; all forty-nine working stalks are reunited before the next line. The competing once-per-line hanging method is discussed at length below, not silently replaced with Zhu’s procedure.

[^j20-remainder]: 奇 has more than one sense here. In “remainder numbers” it is read jī, what is left over, not “odd numbers”—four is among them. Elsewhere 奇 and 耦 distinguish the odd and even symbolic classes of a processing round. 策 denotes counting stalks; the text also uses stalk totals as symbolic numbers. “Counted-through stalks” translates 過揲: the stalks left for reuse after the hanging stalk and lodged remainders have been removed.

[^j20-finger-gaps]: 掛 is “hang”; 扐 is “lodge between the fingers.” With the thumb counted first, the fourth-finger gap is between ring and middle fingers, the third between middle and index; the first hanging stalk occupies the little-finger gap. The explanatory parenthesis counting the five actions belongs to the source. The conventional remainder is 1, 2, 3, or 4: where a handful divides exactly by four, its final group of four is retained as the remainder. The five-year intercalation analogy is the source’s schematic explanation, not a claim that every five-year interval in an actual calendar contains exactly two intercalary months.

[^j20-diagram-key]: The eight retained stalk diagrams use 左 and 右 for left and right portions, 掛 for the hanging stalk, and 扐 for the lodged remainders. In the four combined diagrams, 一, 二, 三 label the first, second, and third processing rounds from bottom to top within each case. Dots represent stalks. Empty cells are part of the transmitted tabulation, not omitted English text. The one stalk initially set aside from fifty is not shown as one of these working remainders.

[^j20-case-counts]: The totals 12, 28, 20, and 4 count sixty-four formal combinations of left-hand remainder classes (four possibilities in each of three rounds). They are not sixty-four hexagrams here. If those remainder classes are equiprobable and independent, the corresponding proportions are 3/16 for old yang, 7/16 for young yin, 5/16 for young yang, and 1/16 for old yin. These proportions depend on that sampling model; the passage does not establish that an arbitrary physical method of dividing a handful realizes it exactly.

[^j20-retain-hanging]: “Do not subtract the hanging stalk” concerns the numerical classification of the second and third rounds: the totals four and eight are divided directly by four. It does not instruct the diviner to omit hanging in those rounds or to return that stalk to the working handful. Only the initial hanging stalk is subtracted when the aggregate removed totals 13, 17, 21, and 25 are converted to 12, 16, 20, and 24.

[^j20-mother-child]: “Mother” names the removed-stalk total as generative basis; “child” the counted-through total. Thus the adjusted pairs are 12/36, 16/32, 20/28, and 24/24, each totaling 48. The correspondence with circle and square is the source’s symbolic geometry, not an exact geometrical identity involving pi. For the source’s own reservation about circumference three, see Juan Seventeen.

[^j20-one-three]: In the young-yang explanation, both consulted transcriptions and historical page image 2011 have 一三, “the single three,” where the parallel construction above would suggest 一一, “the single one.” The English retains the transmitted wording; no unattested character has been substituted. The result is nevertheless explicit: 2 + 2 + 3 = 7. Elsewhere the base’s 掛肋 is read 掛扐, hanging and lodging, as supported by the parallel transcription. [Page image 2011](https://www.eee-learning.com/eeebook/middleyi/2011.png).

[^j20-add-remainders]: The formula “one remains: add three,” and its counterparts, records the complementary right-hand remainder to be joined to the left-hand remainder. It is not an instruction to choose a result or artificially add stalks from outside the handful. Zhu Xi himself complains that the compressed wording can misleadingly sound like deliberate manipulation.

[^j20-voices]: “Master Hengqu” is Zhang Zai; “Master Yichuan” is Cheng Yi; Wang Fusi is Wang Bi; Liu Mengde is Liu Yuxi; Liu Zihou is Liu Zongyuan. Hu Fangping reports earlier attributions and Zhu Xi’s criticisms of their transmission. Those attributions are preserved as reports, not independently certified here. “Recent scholars” and “the old method” are relative to the historical speaker, not to the present reader.

[^j20-cai-three-two]: Cai’s phrase 為奇者三，為耦者二, “three odd cases and two even,” is retained rather than changed to make a single four-case distribution. The closing source note to Part Three explains its mixed reference: the first round has three odd-class cases, whereas each later round has two even-class cases. Cai’s subsequent “transferring four” is an interpretive comparison of two distributions, not an extra physical step in casting.

[^j20-yang-old]: The base and parallel transcription read 陽用老, “yang uses the old,” although the ensuing sixteen cases comprise both twelve old-yang and four old-yin cases. The English retains 陽 and flags the mismatch: the intended generalization appears to concern the use of old rather than young lines in divination. No conjectured 易, “the Changes,” has been silently substituted. Later in this juan the compilers challenge aspects of the rule that only nines and sixes may supply line oracles.

[^j20-counterfactual]: Cai’s forty-nine-stalk example leaves one unused from forty-nine, so only forty-eight enter the first division, not the ordinary forty-nine. Zhu’s explanation below explicitly says this. Its equal old-yang/old-yin totals belong to a counterfactual comparison, not an instruction to set aside two stalks in the adopted fifty-stalk method.

[^j20-two-twelves]: The base and parallel web transcription both have 兩其十一, “two elevens,” in the young-yang counted-through calculation. Historical page image 2024 reads 兩其十二, “two twelves.” The English follows the image: 2 × 12 + 4 = 28. This is an attested correction, not an arithmetic conjecture. [Page image 2024](https://www.eee-learning.com/eeebook/middleyi/2024.png).

[^j20-remainders-priority]: The disagreement here is substantive. Zhu gives the removed hanging-and-lodged counts explanatory priority as the “source,” whereas Gui Youguang and He Kai argue that the counted-through stalks provide the primary numbers, with remainders derivative. The imperial compilers invite comparison rather than simply overriding one side. Both descriptions reproduce the same four outcomes in the adopted procedure.

[^j20-year]: The primer uses a schematic thirty-day month and twelve-month year, then describes 360 as the mean between 366 solar-term days and 354 lunar days. The English retains its stated numbers, not an exact modern astronomical year. The relevant arithmetic is 6 × 36 = 216, 6 × 24 = 144, and 216 + 144 = 360; likewise 6 × 28 + 6 × 32 = 360.

[^j20-operations]: 營 is an operation; 變 here is a processing round, not yet a changing line. Four operations make one round, three rounds one line, and eighteen rounds a six-line figure: 72 operations in all. The base’s 二變而得八卦之象 is corrected to 三變, “three rounds,” following the parallel transcription. The distinction between the symbolic images of successive rounds and the actual strokes produced by completed lines is essential.

[^j20-final-source-note]: This explanatory note appears in the base after the concluding quotation and gloss of Part Three. It is translated as a source note, not attributed to the imperial compilers without evidence. The base’s concluding 奇二而耦二, “two odd and two even,” is retained against the parallel transcription’s erroneous 奇三而耦二.

[^j20-using]: “Using Nines” and “Using Sixes” are the special Qian and Kun statements already translated in Juan One. Here the primer treats them both as a general rule that old lines change and as special oracles for all-changing Qian and Kun. A resulting yin line is represented as young yin, and a resulting yang line as young yang; this does not mean recasting the stalks.

[^j20-judgment]: 彖辭 here means the hexagram Judgment statement beneath the figure, not the separately transmitted Commentary on the Judgments (彖傳). 貞 and 悔 are retained as zhen and hui where the text assigns them: inner and outer trigrams when no line changes; original and resulting hexagrams in the three-changing-line rule. The words must not be collapsed into one fixed pair of English technical meanings.

[^j20-historical-examples]: The brief examples summarize traditional narratives and quotations, not necessarily divinations personally performed by every named speaker. Cai Mo’s material is a reported discussion of Changes passages; Chen Jingzhong is the Chen Wan mentioned in Hu Yigui’s following gloss. The translation retains the differences in how the source describes the examples. Shasui’s Master Cheng is Cheng Jiong, not Cheng Yi.

[^j20-inferred-rules]: The glosses expressly acknowledge that the two- and four-changing-line rules have no stated precedent in the Classic and commentaries and are inferred by analogy. These admissions are retained. The rules are the primer’s proposed interpretive system, not presented as an uncontested method followed by every commentator in the compilation.

[^j20-three-lines]: For any original six-line figure, choosing three changing positions gives twenty possible resulting figures. “The first ten” and “the latter ten” refer to those twenty cases in the chart’s reading order, not to the first twenty King Wen hexagrams. Counting the unchanged starting figure as entry 1, the three-line cases occupy entries 23–42: entries 23–32 privilege the original Judgment, 33–42 the resulting Judgment. The source still calls for consulting both.

[^j20-mujiang]: The primer understands 艮之八 as five changing lines with the second remaining eight. It therefore prefers the second-line statement of the resulting Following. Its charge that the historian wrongly consulted Following’s Judgment is a methodological judgment of the primer, not a conclusion imposed by the translator. The imperial compilers’ closing essay questions this interpretation of 八 and these multiple-changing-line rules more generally.

[^j20-chart-reading]: The base’s 最初卦者 is corrected to 得初卦者, “one who obtains the first figure,” following the parallel transcription. Each chart is reversible between its complementary endpoints. “The thirty-second figure” is the midpoint of that chart’s 64 entries in the direction of reading; it is not hexagram 32, Constancy. The broad chart-reading summary must be read together with the preceding specific rules, including the special all-changing Qian and Kun statements. The final instruction literally names line statements; it is retained as the source’s abbreviated summary and does not cancel the preceding rules that direct the reader to a hexagram Judgment in particular cases.

[^j20-chart-order]: The charts retain all thirty-two groups and all 2,048 displayed figures, in the original 576 paragraph rows. Added entry numbers 1–64 indicate reading positions from each chart’s top; the number in parentheses beside a name is its King Wen hexagram number, not a chart position. Read each row left to right and the rows from top to bottom from the first endpoint; read the whole sequence in reverse from the last endpoint. The figure at entry 1 has no changed line; the other groups have 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 changing lines, in groups of 6, 15, 20, 15, 6, and 1. Within each group, line positions are ordered lexicographically from the bottom line upward. The midpoint divides the twenty three-changing-line cases into two tens. This key is an explanation of the transmitted charts, not a replacement divination method.

[^j20-chart-glyphs]: CORRECTED CHART IMAGES. In ten places the base labels a figure 謙, Modesty (15), but embeds the image for 豫, Enthusiasm (16). The English retains the supported Modesty label and substitutes the repository’s existing Modesty image. The affected chart/entry pairs, numbered from the top, are 6/47, 11/37, 15/31, 18/59, 20/26, 22/24, 25/21, 28/54, 30/16, and 32/14. The parallel chart labels and the specified line transformations agree with Modesty in every case. All original Chinese files and image files remain unchanged; each changed English reference is recorded in the manifest.

[^j20-chart-fifteen]: CORRECTED CHART ENTRIES. Chart Fifteen, Clinging to Repeated Danger, has two mistaken names and images in the base: entry 34 reads 節, Limitation (60), instead of 履, Treading (10); entry 37 reads 大有, Great Possession (14), instead of 泰, Peace (11). The parallel Siku transcription reads 履 and 泰 respectively, agreeing with the chart’s ordered changing-line combinations. Both English labels and image references are corrected here. The original source is untouched; old and new readings and image hashes are recorded in the manifest.

[^j20-jiao]: Jiao Gan and Jiao Yanshou are names for the same figure invoked by the source in connection with the Forest of Changes (易林). “Master Ziyang” here is Zhu Xi. Hu Yigui contrasts the received hexagram sequence used in that work with Zhu’s systematic ordering by the number and positions of changing lines. This reports his account of their relationship, not an independently established history of the charts.

[^j20-compilers-alternative]: The final Editorial Judgment is not a gloss endorsing every rule above. The compilers propose consulting the original and resulting figures’ configurations and Judgments in all cases, with a line oracle singled out when one line alone changes. In their reading, multiple simultaneous changes do not supply separate line oracles. They also question reading every historical 八 as a stationary yin line. Their interpretation and Zhu’s scheme are presented as distinct positions within the compilation; neither is silently substituted for the other.

[^j20-eight]: The closing three examples are Peace’s “eight” (no changing line in the compilers’ reading), Difficulty at the Beginning with Enthusiasm as hui, “all eights” (three changing lines), and Keeping Still’s “eight” becoming Following (five changing lines). The compilers argue that all three use a hexagram rather than a uniquely moving line. Their formula 卦以八成 treats eight as a marker of hexagram formation through the Eight Trigrams. This differs from Cheng Jiong’s explanation of “all eights” above. The “Inner and Outer Commentaries” are the Zuo Tradition and Discourses of the States (國語), the two works explicitly named in the last sentence.
